## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time (400-600 steps): score increases for every moving robot → robots should be **stationary**
- After switch time: score increases for every moving robot → robots should **move** (actually wait, re-reading: "score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving" after switch time → we want robots to be stationary after switch too? No wait...)

Let me re-read: "Before the switch time, score increases by 1 for every robot that is **stationary**. From the switch time onwards, score increases by 1 for every robot that is **moving**."

So to minimize:
- Before switch: robots should be **moving**
- After switch: robots should be **stationary**

The switch is signaled by a GREEN wall lighting up. So robots need to:
1. Move before the switch (exploration/movement)
2. Stop after detecting green

The champion (score 11945) is just pure Exploration (B=0) with rwm=57. This makes sense because:
- Before switch (~500 steps): robots move → good, no penalty
- After switch (~700 steps): robots move → bad, +20 per step × 700 = 14000 penalty... but the champion got 11945

Wait, if a robot stops (B=1), before switch it gets penalized. If it moves always, after switch it gets penalized. The optimal would be to detect the green signal and stop.

The champion (always moving) gets ~11945. The best strategy should be: move until green is visible, then stop.

Let me design machines that:
1. Start in Exploration state
2. Transition to Stop when green (color 2) is perceived via camera (C=7, l=2)

This should dramatically reduce the score.

**Key machines to try:**
- 2-state: Explore → Stop on green detection (C=7, color 2)
- 2-state: Explore with multiple transitions including green detection
- 3-state versions with fallback

**State designs:**

1. **Elitism**: Copy champion
2. **Key insight machine**: Explore → Stop on green (C=7, l=2)
3. Variations of above with different probabilities
4. Stop → Explore on no-green (harder to detect)
5. GoToColor green then stop nearby

Let me build 14 machines:

- 1 champion copy
- 5 exploitations of explore+stop-on-green concept
- 5 explorations with different topologies
- 3 other ideas

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insights from Round 2:**

The champion (score 7674) uses:
- State 0: GoToColor green (B=8, clr=2) → transition to Stop when green perceived (C=7, l=2, p=0.9)
- State 1: Stop → transition with tiny probability (C=5, p=0.002)

This makes sense: robots move toward green, and when they clearly see it, they stop. The "stop when green seen" is the right signal detection.

The 2nd best (8461) uses Exploration (rwm=30) → Stop on green with p=0.95, with a tiny random escape from Stop.

**What works well:**
- GoToColor(green) as initial behavior → best
- Stop as second state with tiny escape probability
- Transitioning on C=7 (perceive green, l=2) with high probability

**What to try:**
1. Keep champion
2. Vary the green-perception probability (0.85, 0.95, 1.0)
3. Vary the escape probability from Stop (0.001, 0.003, 0.005)
4. Try GoToColor with different transition conditions
5. Try 3-state: GoToColor → Stop with multiple transition conditions
6. Try making the Stop state more stable (lower escape prob)
7. Explore: attraction during search phase, then stop on green
8. Try adding neighbour conditions alongside green detection

**Key observation:** The champion's GoToColor(green) ensures robots actively seek the green signal, which makes them more likely to see it and stop. The tiny p=0.002 escape from Stop means robots mostly stay stopped but occasionally restart if green disappears.

Let me note: color 2 = green in the color scheme.

**Corrections:** cle values must be in {0, 4, 5, 6} — no 2 or 3.

## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (score 6183):**
- State 0: GoToColor(green, clr=2) → go to Stop (state 1) when green perceived (C=7, l=2, p=0.9)
- State 1: Stop → with p=0.001 random escape back to state 1 (self-loop? No, n1x0 targets state 1 which is index 1 in "others"... wait)

Let me re-read the champion carefully:
- 3 states: 0, 1, 2
- State 0 (GoToColor green): 1 transition → n0x0=1 (destination index 1 among others [1,2], so state 1), condition C=7 (perceive green), p=0.9
- State 1 (Stop): 2 transitions → n1x0=1 (destination: among others [0,2], index 1 = state 2), C=5, p=0.001; n1x1=0 (destination: state 0), C=7 (perceive green), p=0.05
- State 2 (GoToColor green): 1 transition → n2x0=1 (destination: among others [0,1], index 1 = state 1), C=7, p=0.9

So: Stop can return to GoToColor(state 0) if green is seen (p=0.05) or with tiny random chance go to state 2 (also GoToColor). States 0 and 2 both go to Stop when green seen. This creates a robust "find green → stop" loop.

**Key patterns that work:**
1. GoToColor(green) as primary mover
2. Stop as target state
3. Transition on C=7 (perceive green, l=2) with p~0.9
4. Very small escape from Stop (p~0.001)
5. Having a second GoToColor state as backup seems to help

**Exploitation ideas:**
- Vary p for green perception: 0.85, 0.90, 0.95
- Vary escape probability: 0.0005, 0.001, 0.002
- Add neighbour conditions to Stop state
- Try 4-state versions with more robustness
- Try the 2nd best (2-state with dual transition from GoToColor)

**The 2nd best (6229):** GoToColor → Stop on green (p=0.9) OR random (p=0.001). Stop → random escape (p=0.001). Very simple but effective.

**Exploration ideas:**
- 3-state where Stop can return to GoToColor only if green disappears
- 4-state with attraction in one state for grouping
- Try higher green-detection probability (0.95, 1.0) in the champion topology

Let me carefully construct 14 machines:

(Champion - exact copy)

Now variations:

1. Champion with p=0.95 for green detection
2. Champion with p=0.85 for green detection

7. 3-state champion variant with state 2 having different p
8. 4-state extension
9. Exploration: 3-state with GoToColor → Stop → GoToColor(via green seen)
10. Variant with stop having neighbour condition

## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (score 6167):**
- State 0: GoToColor(green) with 2 transitions: stop when green seen (C=7, l=2, p=0.9) OR random tiny escape (C=5, p=0.001) → both go to state 1 (Stop)
- State 1: Stop with 1 transition: random escape (C=5, p=0.001) back to GoToColor

Key insight: The dual transition from GoToColor (green-detect + tiny random) is slightly better than single transition. The tiny random from GoToColor means if robots get stuck not seeing green, they still eventually proceed to Stop.

**Observations:**
- Scores are tightly clustered around 6167-6687, suggesting we're near a local optimum
- The "return from Stop when green seen" (p=0.05) slightly hurts (rank 6 vs rank 1/2) - it makes robots re-enter GoToColor unnecessarily
- p=0.9 vs p=0.95 for green detection barely matters (6167 vs 6168)
- p=0.001 escape from GoToColor seems optimal

**What to try:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Vary escape probabilities more finely (0.0005, 0.001, 0.0015, 0.002)
3. Try p=0.0 escape (no random, only green-triggered) - but need at least 1 transition, so very tiny p
4. Try different green-detection probabilities
5. Explore: what if Stop has NO exit? (can't - need transitions)
6. Try making Stop escape even smaller (0.0001)
7. Try 3-state where state 2 is also GoToColor but with slightly different params
8. Explore: attraction + stop on green (different search behavior)
9. Try GoToColor with p=1.0 for green detection (always stop when green seen)

**Key principle:** The champion has GoToColor→Stop on green (p=0.9) AND random (p=0.001). The random from Stop (p=0.001) allows recovery. The "return to GoToColor when green seen from Stop" seems to hurt.

Let me try:
- Making the random escape from GoToColor even smaller (0.0005, 0.0001)
- Making the Stop escape smaller (0.0005, 0.0001)
- Various combinations
- Adding a 3rd state for more robustness

## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (score 5486):**
- State 0: GoToColor(green) with 2 transitions: green perceived (C=7, l=2, p=0.9) → Stop, OR random (C=5, p=0.002) → Stop
- State 1: Stop with 1 transition: random escape (C=5, p=0.0005) → GoToColor

Key finding: p=0.002 random escape from GoToColor + p=0.0005 escape from Stop is the best combo found so far. This is better than 0.001+0.0005 (rank 2: 5947) or 0.001+0.001 (rank 5: 6287).

**Pattern:** Higher random escape from GoToColor (0.002) means more robots quickly enter Stop state even without seeing green (before the switch, this helps robots stop sooner). Lower escape from Stop (0.0005) means robots stay stopped longer once stopped (good after switch).

**What to explore:**
- Try p=0.003, 0.004, 0.005 for GoToColor random escape
- Try p=0.0001, 0.0002, 0.0003 for Stop escape (even smaller)
- Try p=0.002 + p=0.0002
- Try p=0.003 + p=0.0005
- Try p=0.002 + p=0.001 (worse direction but confirm)
- Try varying green-detection probability (0.85, 0.9, 0.95)
- Explore 3-state versions of best topology
- Try what happens with even higher random escape from GoToColor (0.005, 0.01)

**Key insight:** The random escape from GoToColor acts like a "timeout" — robots that haven't found green yet still eventually stop. Higher p means faster timeout → fewer steps moving before switch. But too high means robots stop before finding green and then can't respond to it.

The Stop escape (0.0005) should be very small so robots stay stopped after switch. Making it even smaller could help.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (score 4978):**
- State 0: GoToColor(green) → Stop when green seen (p=0.9) OR random (p=0.002)
- State 1: Stop → random escape (p=0.0001) back to GoToColor

**Key trend:** Smaller Stop escape probability is consistently better:
- p=0.0001 → 4978 (best)
- p=0.0002 → 5090
- p=0.0005 → 5459

The smaller the escape from Stop, the longer robots stay stopped after the switch. This makes sense: once stopped, staying stopped is rewarded.

**What to try next:**
- Even smaller Stop escape: p=0.00005, p=0.00001 (approaching zero)
- Try p=0.0 is not allowed (must be in [0,1] but 0 would mean never escape — let's try very small values)
- The GoToColor random escape p=0.002 seems optimal (0.001 and 0.003 are worse)
- Try p=0.002 GoToColor escape + p=0.00005 Stop escape
- Try p=0.002 GoToColor + p=0.00001 Stop escape
- The 3-state version (rank 3: 5104) with Stop→state2(GoToColor) chain is interesting
- Try varying GoToColor escape around 0.002 more finely: 0.0015, 0.0018, 0.0022, 0.0025

**The 3-state machine (rank 3):** Stop escapes to state 2 (another GoToColor) with p=0.0005, which then goes back to Stop. This creates a chain that keeps robots searching if they escape Stop. But the Stop escape is 0.0005 here, which is larger than champion's 0.0001.

Let me focus on:
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. p_stop_escape = 0.00005 (5e-05)
3. p_stop_escape = 0.00001 (1e-05)  
4. p_goto_random = 0.0015, p_stop = 0.0001
5. p_goto_random = 0.0018, p_stop = 0.0001
6. p_goto_random = 0.0022, p_stop = 0.0001
7. p_goto_random = 0.0025, p_stop = 0.0001
8. p_goto_random = 0.002, p_stop = 0.00005
9. 3-state: GoToColor→Stop with p=0.002 random, Stop→GoToColor2 with p=0.0001, GoToColor2→Stop with p=0.002 random
10. p_goto_random = 0.002, p_green = 0.95, p_stop = 0.0001
11. p_goto_random = 0.002, p_green = 0.85, p_stop = 0.0001
12. p_goto_random = 0.001, p_stop = 0.0001
13. p_goto_random = 0.003, p_stop = 0.0001

**Champion analysis (score 4754):**
- 3-state machine:
  - State 0 (GoToColor green): → State 1 (Stop) when green seen (p=0.9) OR random (p=0.002)
  - State 1 (Stop): → State 2 (GoToColor green) with tiny random (p=0.0001)  [note: n1x0=1, destination index 1 among others {0,2} = state 2]
  - State 2 (GoToColor green): → State 1 (Stop) when green seen (p=0.9) OR random (p=0.002)

So: Two GoToColor states both feed into Stop. Stop rarely escapes back to a GoToColor state. This is better than the 2-state version because having two GoToColor states creates more opportunities to reach Stop.

- The 3-state champion (4754) beats the best 2-state (5115) by ~360 points
- The Stop escape p=0.0 (rank 2, score 5115) actually performs well! Zero escape means robots never leave Stop once they enter it

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion (4754) remains unchanged.** The 3-state topology with GoToColor(green)→Stop→GoToColor(green) cycle is clearly superior.

**Key findings from Round 8:**
- p_stop=0 (never escape Stop) scored 4768 — nearly as good as champion's 4754 with p_stop=1e-04
- p_green=0.85 scored 4804 (slightly worse than 0.9)
- p_green=0.95 scored 4851 (slightly worse than 0.9)
- p_goto_random=0.002 is optimal (0.0018 scores 5025, 0.0015 scores 5523, 0.001 scores 6193)
- p_stop=2e-04 scored 4917 (worse than 1e-04)

**Clear trend:** p_goto_random=0.002 is best; p_stop should be as small as possible (0 or 1e-04).

**What to explore next:**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. p_stop=0 (confirmed near-best)
3. p_stop=5e-05 (between 0 and 1e-04)
4. Mix: p_green=0.87, p_goto_random=0.002, p_stop=1e-04
5. Mix: p_green=0.80, p_goto_random=0.002, p_stop=1e-04
6. Mix: p_green=0.9, p_goto_random=0.0019, p_stop=1e-04
7. Mix: p_green=0.9, p_goto_random=0.002, p_stop=0, asymmetric (state 0 and 2 different)
8. 4-state with correct indexing: GoToColor0→Stop1, Stop1→GoToColor2, GoToColor2→Stop1, Stop1 also→GoToColor3, GoToColor3→Stop1
9. p_green=0.9, p_goto=0.002, p_stop=0 with different cle colors
10. Try p_goto_random=0.002 on state 0 but p_goto_random=0.003 on state 2 (asymmetric)
11. Try p_green=0.9 on state 0 but p_green=0.85 on state 2

**For 4-state correct indexing:**
States 0,1,2,3. State 0: GoToColor. State 1: Stop. State 2: GoToColor. State 3: GoToColor.
- State 0 others: {1,2,3} → 0=s1, 1=s2, 2=s3
- State 1 others: {0,2,3} → 0=s0, 1=s2, 2=s3  
- State 2 others: {0,1,3} → 0=s0, 1=s1, 2=s3
- State 3 others: {0,1,2} → 0=s0, 1=s1, 2=s2

GoToColor0 → Stop1: n0x0=0 (dest=s1), n0x1=0 (dest=s1)
Stop1 → GoToColor2: n1x0=1 (dest=s2)
GoToColor2 → Stop1: n2x0=1 (dest=s1), n2x1=1 (dest=s1)
GoToColor3 → Stop1: n3x0=1 (dest=s1), n3x1=1 (dest=s1)

But how does Stop reach GoToColor3? Need two transitions from Stop:
Stop1 → GoToColor2: n1x0=1 (dest=s2) with p=1e-04
Stop1 → GoToColor3: n1x1=2 (dest=s3) with p=1e-05

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
